    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. The 4B router improves at episode level ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$), confirming task-type classification; cross-task-type transfer yields chance accuracy (49.2\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nWhile death dominance follows from the penalty structure (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}), three properties were not predictable a priori: (i)~\\emph{routing prior}---the oracle matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions (2.89-point improvement), and threshold routing with perfect death risk knowledge still cannot outperform always-deterministic (Appendix~\\ref{sec:threshold-routing}), so the rational prior is ``do not route''; (ii)~\\emph{intervention resistance}---retry ($43.5\\%$), Reflexion ($34.0\\%$), $10{\\times}$ scaling ($30.6\\%$), and action-space filtering ($29.0\\%$) all leave ${\\geq}$12~pp residual gaps to deterministic safety ($16.3\\%$), with Reflexion and scaling converging to overlapping Wilson CIs suggesting a shared bound from lacking ground-truth state access; (iii)~\\emph{cross-model stability}---Qwen-7B and Llama-8B show nearly identical internal vulnerability (${\\sim}$42\\%), and 72B scaling leaves death dominance intact (DAF $= 92.4\\%$). More aggressive interventions (Tree-of-Thought, process reward models) remain untested; we characterize a ceiling, not a universal bound.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to misuse of \\texttt{focus on [object]}, the answer-submission action, as a general interaction command (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}). The root cause is grounding failure: agents parse task descriptions correctly but cannot map understanding to valid primitives; the majority of death episodes show effective reasoning in non-fatal steps before a single grounding error triggers irreversible failure. The internal strategy's higher death count (105 vs.\\ 40) reflects more frequent entry into catastrophic states, not different within-episode behavior (${\\sim}5\\%$ \\texttt{focus on} rate in both); lethality depends jointly on misuse rate and answer distribution (\\texttt{find-non-living-thing} has zero deaths despite high submission rate because non-living objects are the majority class). Direct grounding interventions reduce but do not eliminate vulnerability ($48.0\\% \\to 29.0\\%$; Appendix~\\ref{app:grounding-ablation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Practitioner implications.}\nA three-stage diagnostic (${\\sim}$50-episode pilot $\\to$ death-rate differential $\\to$ death-label fraction; Appendix~\\ref{app:decision-framework}) correctly predicts outcomes for all four conditions (Qwen-7B, Llama-8B, Qwen-72B, MATH). Our router training consumed ${\\sim}$200 GPU-hours without exceeding always-deterministic; this diagnostic could have predicted that from a 50-episode pilot. The 4B router's end-to-end score ($-12.03$, below always-deterministic $-9.47$) may partly reflect distribution shift at deployment~\\citep{dagger-2011}.\n\n\\paragraph{Framework applicability.}\nThe penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the primary diagnostic; DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Preliminary pilots on MiniHack~\\citep{minihack-2021} (0\\% death) and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022} (100\\% death, both strategies) produced no death asymmetry, illustrating two boundary conditions: no death mechanism yields no signal; universal death lacks exploitable asymmetry. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks remain the most direct replication target \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024}.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10/11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly because death risk depends on multi-step trajectories. No prior work has quantified signal composition; our finding that a single mechanism accounts for 78.9\\% of routing labels contradicts this implicit diversity assumption.\n\\emph{Intervention resistance.}\nFrom the setup alone ($P{=}100$, internal DR${}=42.9\\%$), one might predict that scaling, self-correction, or action filtering would substantially reduce death vulnerability. {

  "old_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe decomposed routing signal in modular LLM agents: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of non-tie labels arise from death asymmetry, confirmed across four intervention axes and two cross-domain probes. Future work should target intermediate death regimes and stronger backbones.",
  "new_string": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. The framework overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate under sparse rewards) through episode-level counterfactual comparison, and provides both penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics for quantifying signal composition.\n\nApplied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from a single mechanism---death asymmetry---rather than the diverse capability-matching signals assumed by routing literature. This finding is robust across four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction), cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training investment could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.\n\nThe framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes where strategy choice affects survival (NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks), explore step-level signal extraction via death-proximal labeling, and extend the analysis to mixed-strategy routing and stronger backbones."
}
```

    "newString": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe introduced a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. The framework overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\\% tie rate under sparse rewards) through episode-level counterfactual comparison, and provides both penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics for quantifying signal composition.\n\nApplied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals that 78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels stem from a single mechanism---death asymmetry---rather than the diverse capability-matching signals assumed by routing literature. This finding is robust across four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion, $10{\\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction), cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld).   "old_string": "\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\nEpisode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF with Cohen's $d = 3.02$, indicating a large effect size; leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (DAF range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}). An independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp of the primary analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). As a conservative sensitivity analysis, cluster-robust inference treating the $K{=}11$ task types as clusters ($p < 0.001$) confirms significance even under within-cluster correlation; 28-task directional consistency (100\\%) provides further support (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:per-task-ci}).",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Sensitivity and seed robustness.}\nNon-clustered episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for the death-avoidance label fraction, independent of cluster assumptions, with Cohen's $d = 3.02$ (interpret cautiously given $K{=}11$ task types). Leave-one-task-out analysis confirms stability (label fraction range: 75.9\\%--78.9\\%; Appendix~\\ref{app:loto-daf}); an independent seed replication ($N{=}220$) yields DAF $= 89.7\\%$, within 0.7~pp (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}). As a conservative check, wild cluster bootstrap treating each task type as a cluster yields $p = 0.002$; however, with $K{=}11$ clusters this test falls below the $K{\\geq}20$--$40$ threshold recommended for reliable inference~\\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}, so we rely on the non-clustered bootstrap as primary. All 28 death-prone tasks share the same direction (Appendices~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}--\\ref{app:per-task-ci})."
}

```json
{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/experiments.tex",
  "old_string": "\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label) and 60.3\\% are pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged)---a granularity mismatch, not label error---leaving minimal signal for the router. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).",
  "new_string": "\\subsection{Router Training: A Negative Result}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\\sim}$88\\% uninformative signal (60.3\\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level---there is no step-level pattern to learn. Evidence favors a mixture: directional conflict is only 0.3\\% of 2{,}040 training steps (labels are directionally correct in 99.7\\% of informative steps), but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal for the router.\n\nAn implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\\%. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: standard ERM on labels with ${\\sim}$88\\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with our results.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Death-proximal labeling (training only on steps within $k$ steps of death events) is a promising direction for extracting step-level signal from episode-level outcomes. Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations})."
}
```

    "oldString": "\\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside; at step level, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label) and 60.3\\% are pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged)---a granularity mismatch, not label error---leaving minimal signal for the router. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with 4-seed configurations shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$, within seed variance). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at or below chance. The multi-step nature of routing (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes. Training and validation use episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode information leakage.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).",
    "newString": "\\subsection{Router Training: A Negative Result}\n\\label{sec:router-diagnostic}\n\nWe probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The oracle margin (2.89 points) caps routing upside. The near-chance step-level accuracy admits two interpretations: (A)~episode-level labels projected onto steps create ${\\sim}$88\\% uninformative signal (60.3\\% pre-event neutral, 27.4\\% post-event), drowning learnable patterns in noise; (B)~routing signal is genuinely task-type-level---there is no step-level pattern to learn. Evidence favors a mixture: directional conflict is only 0.3\\% of 2{,}040 training steps (labels are directionally correct in 99.7\\% of informative steps), but the granularity mismatch leaves minimal signal for the router.\n\nAn implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA; Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}) trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) shows flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\\%$). At episode level, only 4B exceeds always-deterministic ($59.0 \\pm 1.9\\%$, $p < 0.005$); 0.6B and 1.7B remain at chance. Episode-level independent evaluation yields $47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$, below the majority-vote baseline of 50.0\\%. Training uses episode-level splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:router-details}), ensuring no within-episode leakage. The high uninformative rate connects to noisy-label learning~\\citep{natarajan2013learning}: standard ERM on labels with ${\\sim}$88\\% noise yields near-chance classifiers, consistent with our results.\n\n\\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} The 4B router deployed end-to-end ($N{=}245$) scores $-12.03$ (95\\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$). Death-proximal labeling (training only on steps within $k$ steps of death events) is a promising direction for extracting step-level signal from episode-level outcomes. Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\\%$--$800\\%$) provides no improvement; the $3.7\\%$ internal-preferred base rate makes real-data collection expensive (Appendices~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}--\\ref{app:ablations}).",

  "old_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nDeath avoidance as an implicit routing signal connects to safe RL. Constrained MDPs \\citep{altman1999constrained} formalize safety through state or cost constraints; our death-avoidance mechanism can be viewed as an implicit absorbing-state constraint, where routing to deterministic computation avoids irreversible penalty states. Risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk} optimizes CVaR-type objectives to minimize catastrophic outcomes---our framework diagnoses when and why such risk-sensitivity emerges in routing, rather than prescribing it. Classical safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} require explicit constraint specification; our routing mechanism achieves safety implicitly through computation mode selection, without the agent being aware of the safety objective. \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category, consistent with our finding that death events dominate routing signal. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails."
}

Classical safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} require explicit constraint specification; our routing mechanism achieves safety implicitly through computation mode selection, without the agent being aware of the safety objective. \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category, consistent with our finding that death events dominate routing signal. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\n\\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.\n\\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.\n\\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \\cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.\n\\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nSafe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} enforce constraints in MDPs via risk-sensitive objectives, constrained optimization, or safe exploration; \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category; SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails. These assume the agent can learn to avoid catastrophic states; our results show that computation mode routing already functions as an implicit safety mechanism---the deterministic strategy sidesteps catastrophic states entirely.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "312e8146-05c0-4226-b38b-172a3bd14139",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "ff5a0a43-04c5-4143-bf0d-c9aa2172b397",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "b7fd158f-7271-468a-9e96-bd6194c25189",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.",
    "newString": "Since DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).",

Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}."
        ]
      }
    ],

_source line 4191 · 2026-05-19T16:15:11.260Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GFMNWGpNXpx5u3mJhmijwy`</summary>

```text
35	\paragraph{Environmental scope.}
36	Our primary analysis uses a single environment (ScienceWorld, 11 of 30 task types) with one backbone (Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct); generalization requires further validation. The $-100$ death penalty amplifies DAF; environments with softer penalties would exhibit lower DAF values (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). The 19 excluded task types (63\%) lack routing signal because both strategies typically time out within 30 steps; increasing the step budget could surface non-death-dominated signal that our current setup does not capture. Cross-environment analyses on MATH and ALFWorld (\S\ref{sec:cross-env}, \S\ref{sec:discussion}) provide preliminary supporting evidence but do not conclusively establish generalization. A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct cross-model replication (11 task types, $N{=}220$; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}) shows death dominance replicates with direction consistency 10/10 while vulnerability magnitude varies ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ Qwen $+26.5$~pp), though both models are in the 7--8B parameter range; Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) reduces death rate but does not eliminate death dominance (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); validation beyond 72B remains future work. Our quantitative results (DAF $= 90.4\%$, label fractions) are specific to ScienceWorld; the framework's generality claim is methodological (the diagnostic procedure applies to any environment with asymmetric fatal outcomes), not empirical (the specific percentages may differ across environments).
37	
38	\paragraph{Methodological constraints.}
39	Our internal-reasoning baseline uses single-pass ReAct~\citep{react-2023}. Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} reduces death rate from 42.9\% to 31.4\% (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), but more aggressive self-correction or Tree-of-Thought backtracking~\citep{tot-2023} may further reduce internal death rates. Episode-level labels assign a single routing preference to entire episodes, which may be suboptimal for individual steps (particularly those preceding death events). However, of 2{,}040 training steps only 0.3\% exhibit strict directional conflict (step-level oracle opposite to episode label); the remaining apparent mislabeling consists of episode labels applied to steps where neither strategy has a step-level advantage (60.3\% pre-event neutral). The issue is granularity mismatch---applying correct-direction labels to uninformative steps---not directional error. Training on pure-strategy trajectories also introduces distribution shift at deployment~\citep{dagger-2011}. More broadly, projecting episode-level labels onto 10--50 sequential steps with path-dependent state introduces compounding error: early routing decisions alter the state trajectory, potentially invalidating later labels derived from the episode outcome.
40	
41	\paragraph{Statistical limitations.}
42	Episodes within the same task type are not fully independent because they share environment dynamics, though they use different random seeds. Cluster bootstrap with $K{=}11$ task-type clusters yields wider CIs ($[73.0\%, 100\%]$); with $K < 40$ clusters, these should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008,webb-2023}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, $p < 0.001$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}) and 100\% directional consistency across all 28 death-prone tasks (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}) mitigate this concern.
```

</details>

Reflexion~\\citep{reflexion-2023} (verbal self-reflection between trials) reduces death to 31.4\\% across all 11 tasks by rescuing 29.3\\% of first-trial deaths, but the gap to deterministic remains 15.1~pp; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\\sim}$35\\%. Under Reflexion ($N{=}3$, matched-episode analysis), $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = -0.47$, yielding DAF $> 100\\%$: the deterministic advantage is entirely attributable to death avoidance, and in non-death episodes the self-correcting agent slightly outperforms the deterministic baseline.           " \\label{fig:decomposition}",
          " \\end{figure}",
          " ",
          "-Of 95 non-tie routing labels across 245 episodes, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.",
          "+Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) are ties where routing is irrelevant. Of the remaining 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.",
          " ",
          " Under ScienceWorld's penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the full score gap is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $70.8\\%$ at $P{=}25$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-independent characterization. Given $\\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \\approx 90.3\\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\\delta$, $P$, and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are known.",
          " "
        ]

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "under each computation modality",
    "newString": "under each computation mode",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation modality. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of modality (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and modality $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",

          " ",
          " Of 245 episodes, 150 (61.2\\%) are ties where routing is irrelevant. Of the remaining 95 non-tie labels, 75 (78.9\\%) arise from death asymmetry: one strategy dies while the other survives. This label fraction is penalty-independent---it depends only on which episodes produce asymmetric deaths, not on how death is penalized. Of these 75, 54 are sole-signal labels (episodes where only one strategy produced a non-zero score): both strategies score zero without death, so death avoidance was the \\emph{sole} discriminating signal. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score. At $P{=}0$, the 54 sole-signal labels reduce to ties, leaving 41 non-tie labels of which 21 (51.2\\%) still involve death asymmetry; the labeling function itself is penalty-independent (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}), but the practical routing value of death avoidance requires $P > 0$.",
          " ",
          "-Under ScienceWorld's penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the full score gap is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $70.8\\%$ at $P{=}25$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust, penalty-independent characterization.           "+Under ScienceWorld's penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the full score gap is $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$, episode-level bootstrap, $B{=}10{,}000$). DAF is a derived, penalty-dependent quantity: it varies with penalty magnitude ($50.4\\%$ at $P{=}10$; $70.8\\%$ at $P{=}25$; $82.6\\%$ at $P{=}50$; $90.4\\%$ at $P{=}100$; Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}), confirming that the 78.9\\% label fraction provides the more robust characterization. When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} \\to 0$, DAF $\\to 1$ is algebraic necessity regardless of actual death dominance; conversely, when $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (as in Reflexion $N{\\geq}3$; \\S\\ref{sec:agent-improvement}), DAF exceeds 100\\%, reflecting that death asymmetry more than accounts for the full gap.           " ",
          " \\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}",
          " The internal strategy dies in 42.9\\% of episodes versus 16.3\\% for deterministic (2.6$\\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\\Delta_{\\text{full}} = P \\cdot \\delta + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ makes $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\\delta$ with residual $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \\rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \\leq 25$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \\texttt{det\\_preferred}, 9 \\texttt{internal\\_preferred}, and 150 \\texttt{no\\_preference}."

     Per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards---in ScienceWorld, 97.4\% of steps yield identical scores regardless of computation mode. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework that overcomes this degeneracy by decomposing routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components via paired pure-strategy episodes. Applying this framework to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a penalty-independent result; a derived measure (DAF) reaches 90.4\% at $P{=}100$. Cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B), four intervention axes (retry, self-correction, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction), and boundary probes against contrasting environments (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of this finding. The framework diagnoses when catastrophic failure makes routing signal degenerate, enabling practitioners to assess routing value before investing in router training.

     ```latex
     \section{Conclusion}

     We introduced a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. The framework overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate under sparse rewards) through episode-level counterfactual comparison, and provides both penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics for quantifying signal composition.

     Applied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from a single mechanism---death asymmetry---rather than the diverse capability-matching signals assumed by routing literature. This finding is robust across four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction), cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training investment could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.

     The framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes where strategy choice affects survival (NetHack combat tasks), explore step-level signal extraction via death-proximal labeling, and extend the analysis to mixed-strategy routing and stronger backbones.
     ```

{
  "parentUuid": "df388e92-d918-44ee-8df7-105ac638e2c2",

  "attachment": {

    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/method.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\nOur diagnostic framework quantifies what drives routing signal in interactive environments through three components: (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling that generates routing preferences from paired pure-strategy outcomes (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}--\\ref{sec:labeling}); (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), which decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\\S\\ref{sec:daf}); and (iii)~the Signal Concentration Index (SCI), which measures whether routing signal is concentrated in a few task types or distributed systematically (Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}). Episode-level bootstrap resampling provides 95\\% confidence intervals for all metrics.\n\n\\subsection{Problem Setup}\n\\label{sec:problem-setup}\n\nConsider an interactive environment with observation space $\\mathcal{O}$ and action space $\\mathcal{A}$. An agent policy $\\pi: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{A}$ maps observation histories to actions. We define two computation modes $\\mathcal{M} = \\{{\\tt int}, {\\tt det}\\}$: \\textbf{internal} (${\\tt int}$), where the agent predicts intermediate observations via chain-of-thought reasoning (ReAct~\\cite{react-2023}), with prediction errors compounding across steps; and \\textbf{deterministic} (${\\tt det}$), where the agent receives ground-truth observations from the environment simulator at each step. A \\emph{routing policy} $\\rho: \\mathcal{O}^* \\rightarrow \\mathcal{M}$ selects which computation mode to use at each step; our analysis investigates what signal is available to learn $\\rho$. A reasoning-depth ablation ($n{=}120$) found no significant difference ($p > 0.26$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ablations}).\n\n\\subsection{Counterfactual Episode Collection}\n\\label{sec:collection}\n\nPer-step oracle labeling assigns routing labels by comparing single-step outcomes under each computation mode. Under sparse terminal rewards, this approach degenerates: on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, 97.4\\% of steps produce identical intermediate scores regardless of computation mode (5{,}726 of 5{,}878 steps across 11 prescreened task types), yielding no per-step routing signal. The 97.4\\% tie rate arises because ScienceWorld assigns rewards only at episode termination; intermediate steps update environment state but do not change the score, so a per-step oracle can only distinguish strategies through downstream consequences visible at episode end. Episode-level comparison is therefore the principled granularity for sparse-reward environments, not a fallback from per-step analysis: it directly measures whether one strategy produces better terminal outcomes than the other, bypassing the uninformative intermediate steps entirely.\n\n\\paragraph{Pure-strategy trajectories.}\nFor a task instance $t$ and computation mode $m \\in \\mathcal{M}$, a \\emph{pure-strategy trajectory} is the complete episode obtained by applying modality $m$ at every step:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\tau_m^{(t)} = (o_1, a_1, o_2, a_2, \\ldots, o_T, a_T)\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $T \\leq T_{\\max}$ is the episode length (capped at $T_{\\max} = 30$ steps). Both trajectories $\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}$ and $\\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}$ are executed under identical initial conditions (same random seed, same task variation), so performance differences arise solely from the computation mode choice.\n\nEach trajectory $\\tau$ yields a terminal score $s(\\tau) \\in \\mathbb{Z}$ and a death indicator $d(\\tau) \\in \\{0, 1\\}$. When the agent dies ($d = 1$), the environment imposes a penalty: $s(\\tau) = -P$ where $P > 0$ ($P = 100$ in ScienceWorld). The episode pair produces a tuple:\n\\begin{multline}\n    \\bigl(s^{\\tt int}, s^{\\tt det}, d^{\\tt int}, d^{\\tt det}\\bigr) \\triangleq \\\\\n    \\bigl(s(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, s(\\tau_{\\tt det}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt int}),\\, d(\\tau_{\\tt det})\\bigr)\n\\end{multline}\n\nWe use Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct~\\citep{qwen25-2024} (vLLM) on ScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based environment spanning 30 task types with a $-100$ death penalty. After prescreening, we collect 245 episode pairs across 11 task types (20--40 per type). The framework is applicable to any environment where paired episodes under two computation modes can be collected with binary success/failure indicators. We apply it unchanged to ALFWorld and MATH in \\S\\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\\ref{sec:cross-env}.\n\n\\subsection{Episode-Level Labeling}\n\\label{sec:labeling}\n\nWe define a labeling function $L$ that maps a counterfactual pair to a routing preference:\n\\begin{multline}\n    L\\!\\bigl(\\tau_{\\tt int}^{(t)}, \\tau_{\\tt det}^{(t)}\\bigr) \\;\\rightarrow \\\\\n    \\ell \\in \\{{\\tt det\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt int\\text{-}pref},\\; {\\tt no\\text{-}pref}\\}\n\\end{multline}\n\nThe labeling function is \\emph{death-aware}: it prioritizes survival over score, then compares scores among same-status episodes (Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}; Appendix~\\ref{app:labeling}). ScienceWorld scores are integer-valued, so we use exact (in)equality with no threshold. Figure~\\ref{fig:pipeline} illustrates the overall pipeline; label distributions are reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/pipeline_overview.pdf}\n\\caption{Counterfactual episode collection and analysis pipeline. Each task instance is run under both pure strategies independently, producing episode-level labels via Algorithm~\\ref{alg:labeling}.}\n\\label{fig:pipeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Death Avoidance Factor}\n\\label{sec:daf}\n\nTo quantify the contribution of death avoidance to routing signal, we decompose the mean score gap between strategies. Let $\\delta = \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt int}] - \\mathbb{E}[d^{\\tt det}]$ denote the death-rate differential and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ the mean score gap restricted to episodes where neither strategy dies. Partitioning episodes by death status, death-asymmetric episodes (where only one strategy dies) contribute a per-episode gap of $P + \\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$, where $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}}$ is the surviving strategy's mean score; when $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} \\ll P$ (in our data, $\\bar{s}_{\\text{surv}} = 0.87$, or $0.9\\%$ of $P$), these episodes contribute ${\\approx}\\,\\delta P$ in aggregate. The full gap thus decomposes as:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\Delta_{\\text{full}} \\approx \\underbrace{\\delta \\cdot P}_{\\text{death contribution}} + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}\n\\end{equation}\nThe approximation residual is $29.60 - 29.37 = 0.23$ ($0.8\\%$ of $\\Delta_{\\text{full}}$).\n\nWe define the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF) as the fraction of the full score gap attributable to death avoidance:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\text{DAF} = 1 - \\frac{\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}{\\Delta_{\\text{full}}} \\approx \\frac{\\delta P}{\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}}}\n\\end{equation}%\n\\footnote{When $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}} < 0$ (deterministic strategy scores lower than internal on non-death episodes), DAF exceeds 1; death avoidance then overcompensates for a non-death disadvantage.}\n\nSince DAF $= \\delta P / (\\delta P + \\Delta_{\\text{nd}})$, it increases monotonically with $P$, a mathematical consequence rather than an empirical finding. We therefore report both DAF at the environment default ($P{=}100$) and the penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction as complementary metrics. In ScienceWorld, $P$ affects only post-hoc score calculation; the agent's policy (ReAct prompting) does not include $P$ in its observation, so death rates and $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$ are $P$-invariant by design (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}).\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical inference.}\nWe quantify uncertainty via episode-level bootstrap resampling ($B = 10{,}000$). Each resample draws $N = 245$ episodes with replacement, recomputes all metrics, and derives percentile-based 95\\% CIs. We resample at the episode level (not the step level) because the episode is the unit of counterfactual comparison. Penalty sensitivity analysis across hypothetical $P$ values is in Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}.\n\nWe also measure whether death-avoidance signal is concentrated in a few task types via a Signal Concentration Index (SCI $\\in [0, 1]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}), reported in \\S\\ref{sec:death-decomp}.\n\n\\paragraph{Scope of episode-level analysis.}\nAt step level, 97.4\\% of oracle comparisons yield ties (\\S\\ref{sec:collection}); of 2{,}040 training steps, only 0.3\\% exhibit strict directional conflict with episode labels, with 60.3\\% pre-event neutral (neither strategy advantaged). Episode labels thus apply correct direction to uninformative steps---a granularity mismatch, not label error---explaining why step-level router training approaches chance performance (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).\n",
        "numLines": 73,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 73
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/method.tex"
  },

  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Tool Use and External Computation in LLMs.}\nA growing body of work augments language models with external tools \\citep{tool-learning-survey-2024}, spanning several functional categories.\n\\emph{Self-supervised} approaches learn tool invocation from loss signals: Toolformer \\cite{toolformer-2023} inserts API calls filtered by perplexity reduction; ToolACE \\cite{toolace-2025} curates synthetic data for function-calling accuracy.\n\\emph{Token-level} methods embed tools into the model vocabulary: ToolkenGPT \\cite{toolkengpt-2023} represents tools as special tokens resolved at inference.\n\\emph{Task-level} decomposition routes subtasks to specialized modules: ART \\cite{art-2023} generates tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \\cite{chameleon-2023} composes heterogeneous modules per subtask.\n\\emph{RL-based} methods learn routing policies from reward: ReTool \\cite{retool-2025} discovers when code execution is preferable to chain-of-thought.\nAll focus on \\emph{learning to use tools}; we ask a prior question: \\emph{what signal drives the routing decision?}\nRecent concurrent work sharpens this question: \\citet{tool-overuse-illusion-2026} identify a cognitive-illusion effect where LLMs default to external tools even when internal knowledge suffices, finding that tool overuse correlates with model uncertainty rather than task difficulty. \\citet{when2tool-2026} show tool-call decisions are encoded in hidden states before reasoning, \\citet{tool-use-tax-2026} quantify a ``tool-use tax,'' and \\citet{to-call-or-not-2026} propose a necessity, utility, and affordability framework. Our decomposition provides environment-grounded evidence: in ScienceWorld, 78.9\\% of routing labels trace to catastrophic-failure prevention.\n\n\\paragraph{Adaptive Computation and Routing.}\nAdaptive routing traces back to learned halting policies \\citep{graves2016act}. In the LLM era, CogRouter \\cite{cogrouter-2026} adapts reasoning depth per step, ARES \\cite{ares-2026} selects among reasoning effort levels, Mixture-of-Recursions \\cite{mixture-of-recursions-2025} learns token-level recursion depths, and MTRouter \\cite{mtrouter-2026} addresses multi-turn routing.\nRouteLLM \\citep{routellm-2024} learns routers from preference data to select between strong and weak models.\nThese optimize \\emph{how} to route; we reveal \\emph{why} routing helps: in interactive environments, the dominant signal is catastrophic-failure avoidance.\n\n\\paragraph{Counterfactual Evaluation in RL.}\nPrior work generates counterfactual explanations for RL agents by perturbing individual actions within a fixed policy \\citep{olson2021counterfactual, gajcin2023counterfactual, amitai2024explaining}.\nOur approach differs in both purpose and granularity: we execute two complete episodes under different routing strategies, producing prospective paired comparisons that isolate routing signal \\emph{before} any router is trained.\n\n\\paragraph{LLM Agent Environments and Reasoning.}\nScienceWorld \\cite{scienceworld-2022} serves as our primary testbed; Inner Monologue \\cite{huang2022inner} grounds LLM planning in embodied feedback; Voyager \\cite{wang2023voyager} demonstrates open-ended LLM agents; ALFWorld \\cite{alfworld-2021} provides a complementary household environment; AgentBench \\cite{agentbench-2024} offers systematic evaluation. \\citet{zhou2024webarena} document common failure modes (premature termination, hallucinated actions) in web-based agents, consistent with our finding that state hallucination is the primary internal-strategy failure mechanism. ReAct \\cite{react-2023} serves as our internal baseline; Reflexion \\cite{reflexion-2023} extends this with verbal self-reflection; LATS \\cite{lats-2024} adds tree search. SelfCheckGPT \\cite{selfcheckgpt-2023} detects unreliable LLM outputs; our work shows that in interactive environments, such unreliability manifests as catastrophic death.\n\n\\paragraph{Safe RL.}\nDeath avoidance as an implicit routing signal connects to safe RL. Constrained MDPs \\citep{altman1999constrained} formalize safety through state or cost constraints; our death-avoidance mechanism can be viewed as an implicit absorbing-state constraint, where routing to deterministic computation avoids irreversible penalty states. Risk-sensitive RL \\citep{mihatsch2002risk} optimizes CVaR-type objectives to minimize catastrophic outcomes---our framework diagnoses when and why such risk-sensitivity emerges in routing, rather than prescribing it. Classical safe RL methods \\citep{garcia2015comprehensive,tamar2015cvar,achiam2017cpo,crpo-2021,moldovan2012safe} require explicit constraint specification; our routing mechanism achieves safety implicitly through computation mode selection, without the agent being aware of the safety objective. \\citet{ruan2024failures} provide a taxonomy of LM agent failures identifying catastrophic actions as a distinct category, consistent with our finding that death events dominate routing signal. SafeRoute \\citep{saferoute-2025} applies adaptive model selection for safety guardrails.\n",
        "numLines": 26,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 26
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01WBWtKZ89Xa71fuqtyhUeh6`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We introduced a diagnostic framework for decomposing routing signal in modular LLM agents. The framework overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate under sparse rewards) through episode-level counterfactual comparison, and provides both penalty-independent (label fraction) and penalty-dependent (DAF) metrics for quantifying signal composition.
4	
5	Applied to ScienceWorld, the framework reveals that 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels stem from a single mechanism---death asymmetry---rather than the diverse capability-matching signals assumed by routing literature. This finding is robust across four intervention axes (retry, Reflexion, $10{\times}$ scaling, action-space restriction), cross-model replication (Llama-3.1-8B), and boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld). The diagnostic value is practical: our 200 GPU-hour router training investment could not outperform the always-deterministic baseline, an outcome a 50-episode pilot would have predicted.
6	
7	The framework applies to any environment with episode-level evaluation and binary catastrophic outcomes. Future work should target environments with intermediate death regimes where strategy choice affects survival (NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} combat tasks), explore step-level signal extraction via death-proximal labeling, and extend the analysis to mixed-strategy routing and stronger backbones.
8	
```

### Tool result

_source line 4289 · 2026-05-19T16:20:02.052Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_011pcTD2s3bTZKgYBKrUgWZC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	Large language models deployed as interactive agents must repeatedly decide \emph{how} to compute: reason internally, or delegate to an external tool.
4	A Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct agent navigating ScienceWorld \cite{scienceworld-2022}, a text-based science simulation with 30 task types, faces this choice at every step. Tracking temperature thresholds, container states, and multi-step procedures via internal reasoning is error-prone: the model may hallucinate that water has boiled when the stove was never activated, or that an object has been moved when the action was invalid. Deterministic external computation (environment simulators, code interpreters) eliminates such hallucinations via exact state transitions. The practical question for agent design is therefore: \emph{when should an agent route computation externally versus reasoning internally?}
5	
6	Existing routing approaches frame this as a capability or efficiency decision.
7	Toolformer \cite{toolformer-2023} teaches models to insert API calls when doing so reduces perplexity; ART \cite{art-2023} decomposes tasks into tool-augmented programs; Chameleon \cite{chameleon-2023} selects among heterogeneous modules (search, code, vision) per subtask.
8	More recent adaptive-computation methods route to different reasoning depths \cite{cogrouter-2026,ares-2026} or model tiers \cite{mtrouter-2026}.
9	Concurrent analyses of when and whether to invoke tools~\citep{when2tool-2026,tool-use-tax-2026,to-call-or-not-2026} further presuppose diverse routing signals.
10	The shared assumption is that routing signal stems from diverse sources (accuracy, latency, capability matching) and that a learned router can exploit this signal to improve over any fixed strategy.
11	But no prior work has systematically decomposed what drives routing decisions in interactive environments. Without such a decomposition, the field cannot distinguish settings where diverse routing signals justify learned routers from those where a single failure mode dominates and a fixed strategy suffices.
12	
13	We propose a \emph{formal diagnostic framework} for answering this question. The framework comprises (i)~episode-level counterfactual labeling under pure strategies (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:labeling}), and (ii)~the Death Avoidance Factor (DAF), a penalty-dependent metric that decomposes the routing score gap into death-avoidance and non-death components (\S\ref{sec:daf}). Applied to ScienceWorld, \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry} (75 of 95), a penalty-independent finding that holds regardless of how death is penalized. Under ScienceWorld's specific penalty structure ($P{=}100$), the penalty-weighted DAF reaches $90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$), consistent with the predicted value from observed $\delta{=}0.265$ and $P{=}100$.
14	The internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death (a $-100$ penalty), while the deterministic strategy survives the same episodes.
15	Once death episodes are removed, the performance gap collapses to $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$. A per-task consistency check ($r = 0.97$) confirms that the near-perfect correlation is a mathematical consequence of death dominance when $P$ is constant (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}).
16	
17	This framework addresses a methodological gap: per-step oracle comparison degenerates under sparse rewards (97.4\% of ScienceWorld steps produce identical scores regardless of computation mode; \S\ref{sec:collection}), and keyword-based heuristics fail structurally (\S\ref{sec:baselines}). Our counterfactual approach runs complete episodes under pure-internal (ReAct \cite{react-2023}) and pure-deterministic strategies, then compares episode outcomes. The framework applies to any environment with controllable computation mode comparison and binary catastrophic outcomes.
18	
19	Two cross-context checks probe the specificity of this finding.
20	First, a MATH Level~4--5 evaluation ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic computation mode) confirms no routing signal without catastrophic failure: internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy ($73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$, $p = 0.81$).
21	Second, a cross-model replication with Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct (11 task types, $N{=}220$) shows death dominance replicates across model families with direction consistency 10/10 ($+30.0$~pp vs.\ $+26.5$~pp for Qwen; \S\ref{sec:cross-model}).
22	Four intervention axes test whether death dominance is fragile: retry, Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023}, $10{\times}$ parameter scaling (72B), and action-space restriction all reduce death rates, yet none closes the gap to deterministic safety (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}). Action filtering (blocking the fatal action during exploration) and 72B scaling yield the strongest reductions yet still leave ${\geq}$12~pp residual gaps, results not predictable from the penalty structure alone.
23	An exploratory router (Qwen3 LoRA, 0.6B--4B) yields flat step-level balanced accuracy ($56$--$59\%$); at episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
24	
25	Our contributions: (1)~an \textbf{episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} that overcomes per-step oracle degeneracy (97.4\% tie rate) in sparse-reward environments, enabling routing signal decomposition (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}); (2)~applying this framework to ScienceWorld, we find that \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} stem from death asymmetry---a single mechanism dominates routing signal (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}); (3)~\textbf{cross-model validation} (Llama-3.1-8B), four intervention axes, and cross-environment boundary probes (MATH, ALFWorld) confirm the framework's diagnostic utility and the robustness of the finding (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
26	
27	
28	
```
